{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01ArxxrFPPYSGBmS7A6Gd3kn","type":"tool_result","content":" we inserted 699 Neuropixels probes (see an example of one recording of three trials in Fig. 1i), following a grid that covered the left hemisphere of the forebrain and midbrain, which typically represent stimuli or actions on the contralateral side, and the right hemisphere of the cerebellum and hindbrain, which typically represent the ipsilateral side (Fig. 2a). Recordings were collected by 12 laboratories in Europe and the USA, with most recordings using 2 simultaneous probe insertions. To ensure reproducibility, one brain location was targeted in every mouse in every laboratory, as described elsewhere25. Only sessions with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses. Data were uploaded to a central server, preprocessed and shared through a standardized interface. To perform spike sorting on the recordings, we used a version of Kilosort with custom additions. This process produced 621,733 units (including multineuron activity), averaging 889 per probe. To separate individual neurons from clusters of multineuron activity, we then applied stringent quality-control metrics (based on those described in ref. 15), which identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons, averaging 108 per probe.\n    26→\n    27→Methods\n    28→\n    29→n brief, at the beginning of each trial, the mouse was required to not move the wheel for a quiescence period of 400–700 ms. After the quiescence period, a visual stimulus (Gabor patch) appeared on either the left or right (±35° azimuth) of the screen, with a contrast randomly sampled from a predefined set (100, 25, 12.5, 6 or 0%). A 100-ms tone (5-kHz sine wave) was played at stimulus onset. Mice had 60 s to move the wheel and make a response. Stimuli were yoked to the rotation of the response wheel, such that a 1-mm movement of the wheel moved the stimulus by 4 visual degrees. A response was registered if the centre of the stimulus crossed the  ±35° azimuth line from its original position. If the mouse correctly moved the stimulus 35° to the centre of the screen, it immediately received a 3-μl reward; if it incorrectly moved the stimulus 35° away from the centre, it received a time out. If the mouse responded incorrectly or failed to reach either threshold within the 60-s window, a white-noise burst was played for 500 ms and the inter-trial interval was between 1 and 1.5 s. In trials for which the visual stimulus contrast was set to 0%, the mouse had to respond as for any other trial by turning the wheel in the correct direction (assigned according to the statistics of the prevailing block) to receive a reward, but the mouse was not able to perceive whether the stimulus was presented on the left or right side of the screen. The mouse also received feedback (noise burst or reward) on 0% contrast trials.\n    30→\n    31→Each session started with 90 trials in which the probability of a visual stimulus appearing on the left or right side was equal. Specifically, the 100%, 25%, 12.5% and 6% contrast trials were each presented 10 times on each side, and the 0% contrast was presented 10 times in total (that is, the ratio of the 100, 25, 12.5, 6 and 0% contrasts were set at 2, 2, 2, 2 and 1, respectively). The side (and thus correct movement) for the 0% contrast trials was chosen randomly between the right and left with equal probability. This initial block of 90 trials is referred to as the unbiased block (50:50).\n    32→\n    33→After the unbiased block, trials were presented in biased blocks: in right-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 80% of the trials, whereas in left-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 20% of the trials. The ratio of the contrasts remained as above (2:2:2:2:1). Whether the first biased block in a session was left or right was randomly chosen, and blocks were then alternated. The length of a block was drawn from an exponential distribution with scale parameter of 60 trials, but truncated to lie between 20 and 100 trials.\n    34→\n    35→The automated shaping protocol for training involved two collections of sessions. In the first session, the animals started performing a version of the task without biased blocks and were then progressively introduced to harder stimuli with weaker contrasts as they became progressively more competent. They also experienced a debiasing protocol, which was intended to dissuade them from persisting with just one of the choices. Once they were performing sufficiently well on all non-zero contrasts, they were faced with the biased blocks. When, in turn, performance on those was adequate (including on 0% contrast trials, which are informed by the block), they graduated to recording. Supplementary Figure 8a shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the first and second collections (these were not correlated). Supplementary Figure 8b shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the second collection and the performance during the recording sessions. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_014XFBt5RdJ4pXEvyH7UHTet","type":"tool_result","content":"the time window, the number of bins in that time window (that is, the bin size) and the trial event to which it was aligned depended on the variable that is the target of our regression (Supplementary Table 2). These factors are discussed further below and include a variety of behavioural and task variables: stimulus side, choice, feedback and wheel speed and velocity. Although a session may have included multiple probe insertions, we did not perform decoding on these probes separately because they are not independent. Instead, neurons in the same session and region were combined across probes for our decoding analysis. Decoding was cross-validated and compared with a null distribution to test for significance. A given region may have been recorded on multiple sessions; therefore, in the main figures (Figs. 4–7) the region P value was defined by combining session P values using Fisher’s combined probability test, and the region effect size was defined by subtracting the median of the null distribution from the decoding score and reporting the median of the resulting values across sessions. The P values for all regions were then subjected to FDR correction for multiple comparisons at q = 0.01.\n    67→\n    68→Decoding target variables\n    69→\n    70→Stimulus side, choice and feedback were treated as binary target variables for logistic regression. For stimulus side, trials that had zero contrast were excluded. We used the LogisticRegression module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 20,000 maximum iterations, “l1” penalty, “liblinear” solver and “fit_intercept” set to True. We balanced decoder classes by weighting samples by the inverse of the class frequency, 1/(2Pi,class). Decoding performance was evaluated using the balanced accuracy of classification, which is the average of the recall probabilities for the two classes. Supplementary Figure 9 shows histograms of the regression coefficients for all the variables.\n    71→\n    72→Wheel values (speed and velocity) change over the course of a trial, unlike the previous decoding targets, and we therefore had to treat these target variables differently. We averaged wheel values in nonoverlapping 20-ms bins, starting 200 ms before first wheel-movement time and ending at 1,000 ms after first wheel-movement time. Spike counts were similarly binned. The target value for a given bin (ending at time t) was decoded from spikes in a preceding (causal) window spanning W bins (ending at times t, …, t-W). Therefore, if decoding from n neurons, there were (W + 1)n predictors of the target variable in a given bin. In practice, we used W = 10. To decode these continuous-valued targets, we performed linear regression using the Lasso module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 1,000 maximum iterations and “fit_intercept” set to True. For each trial k, neural activity has size N x T, where N is the number of neurons and T the number of time steps. Recordings are split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps. For each trial, we constructed X \\\\in R^{N x T}  by aggregating spike counts and used it to compute a decoder estimate of the true behavior y \\\\in R^P. y is constant within a trial (“per-trial decoding”); when P = T, y varies across time (“per-timestep decoding”).\\n\\nWe apply our models to 433 IBL sessions,17 covering 270 brain regions and four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, and whisker motion energy.\\n\\n In the IBL experiments, mice rotate a wheel to indicate the location of a visual stimulus, defined as their choice (Figure 1A). For the first 90 trials, the stimulus appears randomly on either side with equal probability; in subsequent trials, it appears predominantly on one side in blocks. Mice adapt their behavior to these changing probabilities, allowing estimation of each mouse’s trial-by-trial prior belief (prior) about stimulus location. Additionally, wheel speed and whisker motion energy near the whisker pad are recorded. Whisker motion energy is computed as the mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within a bounding box anchored between nose tip and eye. Choice and prior are static within a trial, while wheel speed and whisker motion energy are time-varying signals sampled at 60 Hz. See STAR Methods sections “data processing” and “hyperparameter selection” for details.\\n\\nData processing\\n\\nIBL trial-aligned dataset\\n\\nFor choice, we align trials to the stimulus onset, considering neural activity from 0.5 s before to 1.5 s post-onset. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01MHDSmRkUiRRPWirxVTYnmz","type":"tool_result","content":"From \"Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding\"\n\nOur goal is to decode behavior y \\in R^P from spike-sorted, temporally binned spike counts. For each trial k, neural activity has size N x T, where N is the number of neurons and T the number of time steps. Recordings are split into 2-s trials, each divided into 20-ms bins, producing T = 100 time steps. For each trial, we constructed X \\in R^{N x T}  by aggregating spike counts and used it to compute a decoder estimate of the true behavior y \\in R^P. y is constant within a trial (“per-trial decoding”); when P = T, y varies across time (“per-timestep decoding”).\n\nWe apply our models to 433 IBL sessions,17 covering 270 brain regions and four behavioral variables: choice, prior, wheel speed, and whisker motion energy.\n\n In the IBL experiments, mice rotate a wheel to indicate the location of a visual stimulus, defined as their choice (Figure 1A). For the first 90 trials, the stimulus appears randomly on either side with equal probability; in subsequent trials, it appears predominantly on one side in blocks. Mice adapt their behavior to these changing probabilities, allowing estimation of each mouse’s trial-by-trial prior belief (prior) about stimulus location. Additionally, wheel speed and whisker motion energy near the whisker pad are recorded. Whisker motion energy is computed as the mean absolute difference between adjacent video frames within a bounding box anchored between nose tip and eye. Choice and prior are static within a trial, while wheel speed and whisker motion energy are time-varying signals sampled at 60 Hz. See STAR Methods sections “data processing” and “hyperparameter selection” for details.\n\nData processing\n\nIBL trial-aligned dataset\n\nFor choice, we align trials to the stimulus onset, considering neural activity from 0.5 s before to 1.5 s post-onset. For prior, we also align trials to the stimulus onset, including neural activity from 0.6 s to 0.1 s pre-onset. The prior represents the mice’s estimate of the stimulus side probability. We use the same decoding window as in the previous study,27 focusing on the period with minimal wheel movements. Within each trial, we segment neural activity into 50-ms non-overlapping time bins. For each time bin, we bin spike counts using all neurons, sorted by Kilosort 2.5,77 from each session. For dynamic behaviors (wheel speed and whisker motion energy), we select the first movement onset as the alignment event, and decode the behavior starting at the alignment event and ending at 1 s after the alignment event. The neural activity within each trial is binned into non-overlapping 20 ms bins. For each time bin, we similarly bin spike counts using all neurons from each session.\n\nIBL trial-unaligned dataset\n\nWe focus only on wheel speed and whisker motion energy, as variables like choice and prior are only defined during experimental trials, which are periods when stimuli are presented and mice respond for rewards. Unlike the trial-aligned dataset, we do not align neural activity and behavior to a specific event onset. Instead, we segment the continuous Neuropixels recordings into 2-s snippets to construct the trial-unaligned dataset for each session. These snippets cover intervals both during and outside experimental trials, capturing the repetitive, structured behaviors within trials as well as the more spontaneous behaviors outside of trials.\n\nFrom \"A brain-wide map of neural activity during complex behaviour\"\n\nWe trained 139 mice (94 male and 45 female) on the International Brain Laboratory (IBL) decision-making task (Fig. 1a,b). On each trial, a visual stimulus appeared to the left or right on a screen, and the mouse had to move it to the centre by turning a wheel with its front paws within 60 s (Fig. 1c). After an initial 90 unbiased trials, the prior probability for the stimulus to appear on the left or right side was constant over a block of trials at a ratio of 20:80% (right block) or 80:20% (left block). Blocks lasted for between 20 and 100 trials, which were drawn from a truncated geometric distribution (empirical mean of 51 trials). Block changes were not cued. Stimulus contrast was uniformly sampled from 5 possible values (100, 25, 12.5, 6.25 and 0%). The 0% contrast trials, when no stimulus was presented, were assigned to a left or right side following the probability distribution of the block. This allowed mice to perform above chance by incorporating this prior in their choices. Following a wheel turn, mice received positive feedback in the form of a water reward, or negative feedback in the form of a white-noise pulse and a 2-s time out. The next trial began after a delay, followed by a quiescence period during which the mice had to hold the wheel still.\n\nRecordings\n\nIn these mice, we inserted 699 Neuropixels probes (see an example of one recording of three trials in Fig. 1i), following a grid that covered the left hemisphere of the forebrain and midbrain, which typically represent stimuli or actions on the contralateral side, and the right hemisphere of the cerebellum and hindbrain, which typically represent the ipsilateral side (Fig. 2a). Recordings were collected by 12 laboratories in Europe and the USA, with most recordings using 2 simultaneous probe insertions. To ensure reproducibility, one brain location was targeted in every mouse in every laboratory, as described elsewhere25. Only sessions with at least 400 trials were retained for further analyses. Data were uploaded to a central server, preprocessed and shared through a standardized interface. To perform spike sorting on the recordings, we used a version of Kilosort with custom additions. This process produced 621,733 units (including multineuron activity), averaging 889 per probe. To separate individual neurons from clusters of multineuron activity, we then applied stringent quality-control metrics (based on those described in ref. 15), which identified 75,708 well-isolated neurons, averaging 108 per probe.\n\nMethods\n\nn brief, at the beginning of each trial, the mouse was required to not move the wheel for a quiescence period of 400–700 ms. After the quiescence period, a visual stimulus (Gabor patch) appeared on either the left or right (±35° azimuth) of the screen, with a contrast randomly sampled from a predefined set (100, 25, 12.5, 6 or 0%). A 100-ms tone (5-kHz sine wave) was played at stimulus onset. Mice had 60 s to move the wheel and make a response. Stimuli were yoked to the rotation of the response wheel, such that a 1-mm movement of the wheel moved the stimulus by 4 visual degrees. A response was registered if the centre of the stimulus crossed the  ±35° azimuth line from its original position. If the mouse correctly moved the stimulus 35° to the centre of the screen, it immediately received a 3-μl reward; if it incorrectly moved the stimulus 35° away from the centre, it received a time out. If the mouse responded incorrectly or failed to reach either threshold within the 60-s window, a white-noise burst was played for 500 ms and the inter-trial interval was between 1 and 1.5 s. In trials for which the visual stimulus contrast was set to 0%, the mouse had to respond as for any other trial by turning the wheel in the correct direction (assigned according to the statistics of the prevailing block) to receive a reward, but the mouse was not able to perceive whether the stimulus was presented on the left or right side of the screen. The mouse also received feedback (noise burst or reward) on 0% contrast trials.\n\nEach session started with 90 trials in which the probability of a visual stimulus appearing on the left or right side was equal. Specifically, the 100%, 25%, 12.5% and 6% contrast trials were each presented 10 times on each side, and the 0% contrast was presented 10 times in total (that is, the ratio of the 100, 25, 12.5, 6 and 0% contrasts were set at 2, 2, 2, 2 and 1, respectively). The side (and thus correct movement) for the 0% contrast trials was chosen randomly between the right and left with equal probability. This initial block of 90 trials is referred to as the unbiased block (50:50).\n\nAfter the unbiased block, trials were presented in biased blocks: in right-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 80% of the trials, whereas in left-bias blocks, stimuli appeared on the right on 20% of the trials. The ratio of the contrasts remained as above (2:2:2:2:1). Whether the first biased block in a session was left or right was randomly chosen, and blocks were then alternated. The length of a block was drawn from an exponential distribution with scale parameter of 60 trials, but truncated to lie between 20 and 100 trials.\n\nThe automated shaping protocol for training involved two collections of sessions. In the first session, the animals started performing a version of the task without biased blocks and were then progressively introduced to harder stimuli with weaker contrasts as they became progressively more competent. They also experienced a debiasing protocol, which was intended to dissuade them from persisting with just one of the choices. Once they were performing sufficiently well on all non-zero contrasts, they were faced with the biased blocks. When, in turn, performance on those was adequate (including on 0% contrast trials, which are informed by the block), they graduated to recording. Supplementary Figure 8a shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the first and second collections (these were not correlated). Supplementary Figure 8b shows a joint histogram of the number of sessions the mice took in the second collection and the performance during the recording sessions. These were also not correlated.\n\nInclusion criteria\n\nWe applied a set of inclusion criteria to sessions, probes and neurons to ensure data quality. Supplementary Table 1 lists the consequences of these criteria for the number of sessions and probes that passed the criteria.\nSessions and insertions\n\nEach Neuropixels insertion was repeated in at least two laboratories, with reproducibility of outcomes across laboratories verified with extensive analyses that we have previously reported25.\n\nSessions were included in the data release if the mice performed at least 250 trials, with a performance of at least 90% correct on 100% contrast trials for both left and right blocks, and, to be able to analyse the feedback variable, if there were at least 3 trials with incorrect choices (after applying the trial exclusions below). Furthermore, sessions were included in the release only if they reached threshold on a collection of hardware tests (definitions are available from GiHub (https://int-brain-lab.github.io/iblenv/_autosummary/ibllib.qc.task_metrics.html)).\n\nInsertions were excluded if the neural data failed the whole recording per visually assessed criteria of the ‘Recording Inclusion metrics and Guidelines for Optimal Reproducibility’ (RIGOR) from ref. 25, by presenting major artefacts (see examples in ref. 28) or if the probe tract could not be recovered during the histology procedure. Furthermore, only insertions for which alignments had been resolved (see appendix 6 of ref. 25 for definitions) were used in this study.\n\nAfter applying these criteria, a total of 459 sessions, 699 insertions and 621,733 neurons remained, constituting the publicly released dataset.\nTrials\n\nFor the analyses presented here, trials were excluded if one of the following trial events could not be detected: choice, probabilityLeft, feebackType, feeback times, stimON times and firstMovement times. Trials were further excluded if the time between stimulus onset and the first movement of the wheel (the first wheel-movement time) were outside the range of 0.08–2.00 s.\nNeurons and brain regions\n\nNeurons generated by the spike-sorting pipeline were excluded from the analyses presented here if they failed one of the three criteria described in ref. 28 (the single unit computed metrics of RIGOR25): amplitude > 50 μV; noise cut-off < 20 μV; and refractory period violation. Neurons that passed these criteria were termed well-isolated neurons (or often just ‘neurons’) in this study. Out of the 621,733 units collected, 75,708 were considered well-isolated neurons. Final analyses were additionally restricted to regions that were designated grey matter in the adult mouse Allen Common Coordinate framework6, contained at least five well-isolated neurons per session and were recorded from in at least two such sessions.\n\nVideo analysis\n\nWe briefly describe the video analysis pipeline (full details can be found in ref. 105). The recording rigs contained three cameras: one called ‘left’ at full resolution (1,280 × 1,024) and 60 Hz, filming the mouse from one side; one called ‘right’, filming the mouse symmetrically from the other side at half resolution (640 × 512) and 150 Hz; and one called ‘body’ at half resolution and 30 Hz, filming the body of the mouse from above. We developed several quality-control metrics to detect raw video issues such as poor illumination (as infrared light bulbs broke) or accidental misplacement of the cameras105.\n\nWe computed the motion energy (the mean across pixels of the absolute value of the difference between adjacent frames) of the whisker pad areas in the ‘left’ and ‘right’ videos (Fig. 1d). The whisker pad area was empirically defined using a rectangular bounding box anchored between the nose tip and the eye, both found using DeepLabCut106 (DLC; see more below). This metric quantifies motion in the whisker pad area and has a temporal resolution of the respective camera.\n\nWe also performed markerless pose estimation of body parts using DLC31, which is used in a fully automated pipeline in IBL (v.2.1) to track various body parts such as the paws, nose, tongue and pupil (Fig. 1d). In all analyses using DLC estimates, we excluded predictions with likelihood < 0.9. Furthermore, we developed several quality-control metrics for the DLC traces105.\n\nOverview of decoding\n\nWe performed a decoding analysis to measure how much information the activity of populations of neurons contained about task variables such as stimulus side and choice. To do this we, used cross-validated, maximum-likelihood regression with L1 regularization (to zero out the contribution from noisy neurons). The neural regressors were defined by binning the spike counts from each neuron in each session in a given region in a specific time window on each trial. The duration of the time window, the number of bins in that time window (that is, the bin size) and the trial event to which it was aligned depended on the variable that is the target of our regression (Supplementary Table 2). These factors are discussed further below and include a variety of behavioural and task variables: stimulus side, choice, feedback and wheel speed and velocity. Although a session may have included multiple probe insertions, we did not perform decoding on these probes separately because they are not independent. Instead, neurons in the same session and region were combined across probes for our decoding analysis. Decoding was cross-validated and compared with a null distribution to test for significance. A given region may have been recorded on multiple sessions; therefore, in the main figures (Figs. 4–7) the region P value was defined by combining session P values using Fisher’s combined probability test, and the region effect size was defined by subtracting the median of the null distribution from the decoding score and reporting the median of the resulting values across sessions. The P values for all regions were then subjected to FDR correction for multiple comparisons at q = 0.01.\n\nDecoding target variables\n\nStimulus side, choice and feedback were treated as binary target variables for logistic regression. For stimulus side, trials that had zero contrast were excluded. We used the LogisticRegression module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 20,000 maximum iterations, “l1” penalty, “liblinear” solver and “fit_intercept” set to True. We balanced decoder classes by weighting samples by the inverse of the class frequency, 1/(2Pi,class). Decoding performance was evaluated using the balanced accuracy of classification, which is the average of the recall probabilities for the two classes. Supplementary Figure 9 shows histograms of the regression coefficients for all the variables.\n\nWheel values (speed and velocity) change over the course of a trial, unlike the previous decoding targets, and we therefore had to treat these target variables differently. We averaged wheel values in nonoverlapping 20-ms bins, starting 200 ms before first wheel-movement time and ending at 1,000 ms after first wheel-movement time. Spike counts were similarly binned. The target value for a given bin (ending at time t) was decoded from spikes in a preceding (causal) window spanning W bins (ending at times t, …, t-W). Therefore, if decoding from n neurons, there were (W + 1)n predictors of the target variable in a given bin. In practice, we used W = 10. To decode these continuous-valued targets, we performed linear regression using the Lasso module from scikit-learn111 (v.1.1.2) with 0.001 tolerance, 1,000 maximum iterations and “fit_intercept” set to True. Decoding performance was evaluated using the R2 metric.","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01BTFa3Me3ZBHcBrTCdJhf5s","session_id":"621b3579-4913-413d-abc7-9ef3a4534867","uuid":"e151417e-b525-473c-87dd-8d35cbbb0151","timestamp":"2026-08-03T14:37:44.418Z"}

KEY FUNCTIONS AND THEIR SIGNATURES\\n\\n### A. DATA LOADING FROM ONE CACHE\\n\\n#### File: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py`\\n\\n**1. `load_spiking_data(one, pid, compute_metrics=False, qc=None, **kwargs)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Load spike trains and cluster information for a probe insertion\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `one`: ONE instance (local or remote database)\\n  - `pid`: Probe insertion UUID\\n  - `compute_metrics`: Force recompute cluster metrics (default: False)\\n  - `qc`: Quality threshold (None = all clusters, 1.0 = only good units)\\n  - `**kwargs`: Additional parameters (eid, pname for offline mode)\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `selected_spikes`: Dict with keys ['times', 'clusters', 'amps', 'depths']\\n  - `selected_clusters`: pandas DataFrame with cluster info\\n  - `sampling_freq`: Sampling frequency (typically 30 kHz)\\n- **Key Details**: Uses SpikeSortingLoader from brainbox.io.one\\n\\n**2. `merge_probes(spikes_list, clusters_list)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Merge spike/cluster data from multiple probes as single recording\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `spikes_list`: List of spike dicts from each probe\\n  - `clusters_list`: List of cluster DataFrames\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `merged_spikes`: Dict with merged spike data (time-sorted)\\n  - `merged_clusters`: DataFrame with re-indexed clusters\\n- **Note**: Uses column 'uuids' for unambiguous cluster identification across probes\\n\\n**3. `load_trials_and_mask(one, eid, min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2.0, nan_exclude='default', ...)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Load all trials and create Boolean mask for trial inclusion/exclusion\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `one`: ONE instance\\n  - `eid`: Session UUID\\n  - `min_rt`, `max_rt`: Reaction time bounds (seconds)\\n  - `nan_exclude`: List of events that cannot be NaN\\n  - `min_trial_len`, `max_trial_len`: Trial duration bounds\\n  - `exclude_unbiased`: Exclude unbiased block (probabilityLeft == 0.5)\\n  - `exclude_nochoice`: Exclude trials where animal didn't respond (choice == 0)\\n  - `sess_loader`: Optional SessionLoader object\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `trials`: pandas DataFrame with all trial events\\n  - `mask`: Boolean Series (True = include trial, False = exclude)\\n- **Default NaN Exclusion Events**: `['stimOn_times', 'choice', 'feedback_times', 'probabilityLeft', 'firstMovement_times', 'feedbackType']`\\n- **Typical Trial Duration Filter**: max_trial_len=10.0 seconds\\n\\n**4. `prepare_data(one, eid, bwm_df, params, n_workers=os.cpu_count())`**\\n- **Purpose**: Main orchestration function that loads all data for a session\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `one`: ONE instance\\n  - `eid`: Session UUID\\n  - `bwm_df`: Brain-Wide-Map DataFrame with session metadata\\n  - `params`: Dict with 'interval_len', 'binsize', 'single_region', 'align_time', 'time_window'\\n  - `n_workers`: Number of parallel processes\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `neural_dict`: Contains spike_times, spike_clusters, cluster_regions\\n  - `behave_dict`: Behavioral data (wheel, motion energy, pupil, etc.)\\n  - `meta_data`: Session metadata (eid, probe_name, sampling_freq, cluster info)\\n  - `trials_data`: Contains trials_df and trials_mask\\n- **Process**:\\n  1. Gets probe IDs from eid using `one.eid2pid()`\\n  2. Loads spike sorting for each probe via `load_spiking_data()`\\n  3. Merges probes via `merge_probes()`\\n  4. Loads trials via `load_trials_and_mask()`\\n  5. Loads continuous behavioral signals via `load_anytime_behaviors()`\\n\\n---\\n\\n### B. BRAIN REGION SELECTION\\n\\n#### File: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py`\\n\\n**5. `list_brain_regions(neural_dict, **kwargs)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Extract unique brain regions from neural data\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `neural_dict`: Contains 'cluster_regions' from spike sorting\\n  - `**kwargs`: Must include 'single_region' (bool)\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `regions`: List of brain regions (format depends on single_region flag)\\n    - If `single_region=True`: List of individual regions as [[reg1], [reg2], ...]\\n    - If `single_region=False`: Single list of all regions [[reg1, reg2, ...]]\\n  - `beryl_reg`: Beryl-mapped region acronyms\\n- **Mapping**: Uses `iblatlas.regions.BrainRegions.acronym2acronym(mapping='Beryl')`\\n- **Printed Output**: \\\"Use spikes from brain regions: [list of regions]\\\"\\n\\n**6. `select_brain_regions(regressors, beryl_reg, region, **kwargs)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Filter clusters by brain region\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `regressors`: (variable name, appears to be unused)\\n  - `beryl_reg`: Beryl region acronyms\\n  - `region`: List of region acronyms to include\\n  - `**kwargs`: Additional arguments\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `reg_clu_ids`: Array of cluster indices matching selected regions\\n\\n---\\n\\n### C. NEURAL DATA BINNING\\n\\n#### File: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py`\\n\\n**7. `bin_spiking_data(reg_clu_ids, neural_df, intervals=None, trials_df=None, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Bin spike data into time windows, typically trial-aligned\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `reg_clu_ids`: Array of cluster IDs to include\\n  - `neural_df`: Dict with spike_times, spike_clusters, cluster_regions, cluster_qc, cluster_df\\n  - `intervals`: Time intervals (alternative to trials_df)\\n  - `trials_df`: pandas DataFrame with trial events\\n  - `n_workers`: Parallel processes\\n  - **kwargs must include**:\\n    - `align_time`: Event to align to (e.g., 'stimOn_times', 'firstMovement_times', 'feedback_times')\\n    - `time_window`: Tuple (start, end) relative to align_time (e.g., (-0.5, 1.5) = 500ms before to 1500ms after)\\n    - `binsize`: Bin width in seconds (e.g., 0.02 = 20ms bins)\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `binned_list`: List of 2D arrays, each shape (n_bins, n_clusters) for one trial\\n  - `clusters_used_in_bins`: Unique cluster IDs\\n- **Binning Strategy**:\\n  - If time_window duration == binsize: Single spike count per trial (1 bin)\\n  - If time_window duration > binsize: Multiple bins via `get_spike_data_per_interval()`\\n- **Details**:\\n  - Uses `iblutil.numerical.bincount2D()` for efficient spike binning\\n  - Handles NaN alignment times gracefully\\n  - Bins contain spike counts (not spike times)\\n\\n**8. `get_spike_data_per_interval(times, clusters, interval_begs, interval_ends, interval_len, binsize, n_workers=os.cpu_count())`**\\n- **Purpose**: Core binning function for multi-bin windows\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `times`: Spike times (seconds)\\n  - `clusters`: Cluster IDs for each spike\\n  - `interval_begs`: Start time of each interval\\n  - `interval_ends`: End time of each interval\\n  - `interval_len`: Duration of each interval\\n  - `binsize`: Width of each bin\\n  - `n_workers`: Parallel processes\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `binned_spikes`: 3D array (n_intervals, n_clusters, n_bins)\\n- **Implementation**: Uses multiprocessing with `bincount2D()` for efficiency\\n\\n---\\n\\n### D. BEHAVIORAL DATA LOADING AND BINNING\\n\\n#### File: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py`\\n\\n**9. `load_target_behavior(one, eid, target)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Load a specific behavioral signal from ONE cache\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `one`: ONE instance\\n  - `eid`: Session UUID\\n  - `target`: Behavior type (see list below)\\n- **Supported Targets**:\\n  - `'wheel-position'`, `'wheel-velocity'`, `'wheel-speed'`\\n  - `'left-whisker-motion-energy'`, `'right-whisker-motion-energy'`\\n  - `'left-pupil-diameter'`, `'right-pupil-diameter'`\\n  - `'left-camera-{left,right}-paw-speed'`, `'right-camera-{left,right}-paw-speed'`\\n  - `'left-nose-speed'`, `'right-nose-speed'`\\n- **Returns**: Dict with keys:\\n  - `'times'`: Timestamps (array)\\n  - `'values'`: Signal values (array)\\n  - `'skip'`: True if error loading data (optional)\\n- **Data Sources**:\\n  - Wheel: SessionLoader.load_wheel()\\n  - Motion energy: SessionLoader.load_motion_energy()\\n  - Pupil/DLC: one.load_object() for camera data\\n  - Speed calculations: brainbox.behavior.dlc.get_speed()\\n\\n**10. `bin_behaviors(one, eid, behaviors, intervals=None, trials_only=False, trials_df=None, mask=None, allow_nans=True, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Bin behavioral signals across trials\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `one`: ONE instance\\n  - `eid`: Session UUID\\n  - `behaviors`: List of behavior names to bin\\n  - `intervals`: Time intervals (alternative to trials_df)\\n  - `trials_df`: DataFrame with trial timing information\\n  - `mask`: Boolean mask to filter trials\\n  - `allow_nans`: Whether to allow NaN values in output (default: True)\\n  - `n_workers`: Parallel processes\\n  - **kwargs**: Same as bin_spiking_data (align_time, time_window, binsize)\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `behave_dict`: Dict mapping behavior names to binned data\\n  - `mask_dict`: Dict mapping behavior names to quality masks\\n- **Special Handling** (called before bin_spiking_data):\\n  - `'choice'`: From trials_df['choice']\\n  - `'block'`: From trials_df['probabilityLeft']\\n  - `'reward'`: From (trials_df['rewardVolume'] > 1).astype(int)\\n  - `'contrast'`: Computed from contrastLeft and contrastRight\\n  - `'whisker-motion-energy'`: Tries left first, then right\\n  - `'pupil-diameter'`: Tries left first, then right\\n\\n**11. `get_behavior_per_interval(target_times, target_vals, intervals=None, trials_df=None, allow_nans=False, n_workers=os.cpu_count(), **kwargs)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Bin continuous behavioral signals to match trial structure\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `target_times`: Timestamps of behavior signal\\n  - `target_vals`: Signal values\\n  - `intervals`: Time intervals\\n  - `trials_df`: Trial DataFrame\\n  - `allow_nans`: Allow NaN values (default: False)\\n  - `n_workers`: Parallel processes\\n  - **kwargs**: align_time, time_window, binsize\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `target_times_list`: List of interpolated times per trial\\n  - `target_vals_list`: List of interpolated values per trial\\n  - `good_interval`: Boolean mask (True = usable trial)\\n  - `skip_reasons`: List of reasons why trials were skipped\\n- **Interpolation Details**:\\n  - Uses `scipy.interpolate.interp1d()` with kind='linear', fill_value='extrapolate'\\n  - Interpolates to uniform sampling: `np.linspace(interval_beg + binsize, interval_end, n_bins)`\\n  - Checks for data availability (start/end within binsize of trial boundaries)\\n\\n**12. `load_anytime_behaviors(one, eid, n_workers=os.cpu_count())`**\\n- **Purpose**: Load all continuous behavioral signals for a session in parallel\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `one`: ONE instance\\n  - `eid`: Session UUID\\n  - `n_workers`: Parallel processes\\n- **Returns**: Dict mapping behavior names to dicts with 'times' and 'values'\\n- **Behaviors Loaded**: wheel-velocity, wheel-speed, whisker motion energy (left/right), pupil diameter (left/right)\\n\\n---\\n\\n### E. TRIAL ALIGNMENT\\n\\n#### File: `/app/code/code_zhang2025/src/utils/ibl_data_utils.py`\\n\\n**13. `align_spike_behavior(binned_spikes, binned_behaviors, beh_names, trials_mask=None)`**\\n- **Purpose**: Ensure neural and behavioral data are aligned (same number of trials, valid samples)\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `binned_spikes`: Array from bin_spiking_data()\\n  - `binned_behaviors`: Dict of arrays from bin_behaviors()\\n  - `beh_names`: List of behavior names to check\\n  - `trials_mask`: Boolean mask from load_trials_and_mask()\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `aligned_binned_spikes`: Filtered spikes array (only valid trials)\\n  - `aligned_binned_behaviors`: Filtered behavior dicts\\n- **Filtering Logic**:\\n  1. Create target_mask = ones for all trials\\n  2. For each behavior: mask out trials where that behavior is None\\n  3. Apply trials_mask if provided\\n  4. Delete rows where target_mask == 0\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 3. IBL DATA LOADING (brainbox library)\\n\\n### File: `/app/code/ibllib/brainbox/io/one.py`\\n\\n#### SpikeSortingLoader Class\\n**Purpose**: Load spike sorting data from ONE cache with automatic probe/sorter selection\\n\\n**Key Methods**:\\n\\n**`load_spike_sorting(spike_sorter='iblsorter', revision=None, enforce_version=False, good_units=False, namespace=None)`**\\n- Loads spikes, clusters, and channels for a probe insertion\\n- Automatically selects best spike sorter (pykilosort > iblsorter)\\n- Returns spikes, clusters, channels\\n\\n**`download_spike_sorting_object(obj, spike_sorter=None, dataset_types=None, collection=None)`**\\n- Downloads spike sorting objects from ONE cache\\n- Accepts 'spikes', 'clusters', 'channels', 'passingSpikes'\\n\\n**`merge_clusters(spikes, clusters, channels, cache_dir=None, compute_metrics=False)`**\\n- Merges metrics and channel information into cluster DataFrame\\n- Computes quality metrics via `quick_unit_metrics()`\\n- Adds keys: all metrics columns, plus channel information (x, y, z, acronym, atlas_id, etc.)\\n\\n#### SessionLoader Class\\n**Purpose**: Load behavioral and trial data with standardized methods\\n\\n**Key Methods**:\\n\\n**`load_trials(collection=None)`**\\n- Loads trial events into SessionLoader.trials DataFrame\\n- Excludes itiDuration (mismatched dimensions)\\n\\n**`load_wheel(fs=1000, corner_frequency=20, order=8, collection=None)`**\\n- Interpolates wheel position to uniform sampling rate (default 1000 Hz)\\n- Computes velocity and acceleration with Butterworth filter (default order=8, cutoff=20 Hz)\\n- Returns DataFrame with columns: times, position, velocity, acceleration\\n\\n**`load_motion_energy(views=['left', 'right', 'body'])`**\\n- Loads motion energy for whisker pad (left/right) or body\\n- Returns dict of DataFrames, one per camera view\\n\\n**`load_pose(likelihood_thr=0.9, views=['left', 'right', 'body'], tracker='dlc')`**\\n- Loads DeepLabCut tracking results\\n- Masks out estimates with likelihood < likelihood_thr\\n- Returns dict of DataFrames, one per camera view\\n\\n**`load_pupil(snr_thresh=5.0)`**\\n- Loads pupil diameter from left/right camera DLC data\\n- Applies SNR threshold to filter outliers\\n\\n#### Helper Functions\\n\\n**`get_spike_counts_in_bins(spike_times, spike_clusters, intervals)`** (from brainbox.population.decode)\\n- **Purpose**: Count spikes in time intervals for each neuron\\n- **Parameters**:\\n  - `spike_times`: 1D array of spike times (sorted)\\n  - `spike_clusters`: 1D array of cluster IDs\\n  - `intervals`: 2D array shape (n_events, 2) with [start, end] times\\n- **Returns**:\\n  - `counts`: 2D array (n_neurons, n_events)\\n  - `cluster_ids`: 1D array of unique cluster IDs\\n\\n---\\n\\n## 4. The BWM CSV has one row per probe insertion (699 rows). Grouping by `eid` yields 459 unique sessions, matching the data paper's count.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. For each session, trials are loaded from `_ibl_trials.table.pqt` (parquet format). Each row in the trials table is one trial. After filtering (see 1-e), each valid trial becomes one entry in the session's trial list.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_trials(alf_path):\n    trials_file = find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_trials.table.pqt')\n    trials = pd.read_parquet(trials_file)\n    return trials\n```\n\niii. This matches the reference code which also loads trials from the same parquet files via SessionLoader.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered in two stages: (1) a trial mask excluding trials with NaN in key events, bad reaction times, no-choice trials, and excessively long trials; (2) a combined mask additionally requiring valid wheel speed and whisker motion energy data for each trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nNAN_EXCLUDE = ['stimOn_times', 'choice', 'feedback_times',\n               'probabilityLeft', 'firstMovement_times', 'feedbackType']\n\ndef create_trial_mask(trials):\n    mask = pd.Series(True, index=trials.index)\n    for event in NAN_EXCLUDE:\n        if event in trials.columns:\n            mask &= ~trials[event].isna()\n    rt = trials['firstMovement_times'] - trials['stimOn_times']\n    mask &= (rt >= MIN_RT) & (rt <= MAX_RT)   # 0.08 <= RT <= 2.0\n    mask &= (trials['choice'] != 0)\n    if 'goCue_times' in trials.columns:\n        trial_len = trials['feedback_times'] - trials['goCue_times']\n        mask &= (trial_len <= MAX_TRIAL_LEN) | trial_len.isna()  # <= 10s\n    return mask\n\n# Second stage: require valid wheel and ME data\ncombined_mask = np.ones(len(valid_trials), dtype=bool)\nif wheel_speed_binned is not None:\n    combined_mask &= ~np.any(np.isnan(wheel_speed_binned), axis=1)\nelse:\n    combined_mask[:] = False\nif me_binned is not None:\n    combined_mask &= ~np.any(np.isnan(me_binned), axis=1)\nelse:\n    combined_mask[:] = False\n```\n\niii. The first stage exactly matches the reference code's `load_trials_and_mask()` with default parameters (min_rt=0.08, max_rt=2.0, max_trial_len=10.0, exclude_nochoice=True, default nan_exclude list). The second stage mirrors the reference code's `align_spike_behavior()` which removes trials without valid behavioral data.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from `spikes.times.npy` (spike timestamps) and `spikes.clusters.npy` (cluster assignments) loaded from each probe's pykilosort directory, plus `channels.brainLocationIds_ccf_2017.npy` for brain region mapping.\n\nii.\n```python\nspikes = {\n    'times': np.load(rev_dir / 'spikes.times.npy').flatten(),\n    'clusters': np.load(rev_dir / 'spikes.clusters.npy').flatten(),\n}\nclusters_channels = np.load(rev_dir / 'clusters.channels.npy').flatten()\n```\n\niii. Same source variables as the reference code's `SpikeSortingLoader.load_spike_sorting()`.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. Spikes from multiple probes are merged (cluster IDs offset, time-sorted). Spikes are then binned into trial-aligned 20ms windows over a 2-second window (-0.5s to 1.5s relative to stimulus onset), producing shape (n_clusters, 100) per trial. The spike counts are stored as uint8.\n\nii.\n```python\nBINSIZE = 0.02  # 20 ms\nTIME_WINDOW = (-0.5, 1.5)\nN_BINS = int(np.ceil((TIME_WINDOW[1] - TIME_WINDOW[0]) / BINSIZE))  # 100\n\ndef bin_spikes_vectorized(spike_times, spike_clusters, n_clusters, interval_starts, interval_ends, binsize, n_bins):\n    binned = np.zeros((n_trials, n_clusters, n_bins), dtype=np.float32)\n    for trial_idx in range(n_trials):\n        idx_beg = np.searchsorted(spike_times, t_start, side='left')\n        idx_end = np.searchsorted(spike_times, t_end, side='left')\n        bin_idx = np.minimum(((t_sel - t_start) / binsize).astype(np.int32), n_bins - 1)\n        np.add.at(binned[trial_idx].ravel(), flat_idx, 1)\n    return binned\n```\n\niii. The binning approach matches the reference code's `get_spike_data_per_interval()` which also produces (n_clusters, n_bins) per trial with 20ms bins. The AI used searchsorted + flat indexing for efficiency rather than the reference's `bincount2D`.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No neuron quality filtering is applied. All clusters from pykilosort are included regardless of their quality label (equivalent to `qc=None` in the reference code).\n\nii.\n```python\n# No filtering code - all clusters loaded and used\nspikes, clusters = merge_probes(spikes_list, clusters_list)\nn_clusters = len(clusters['channels'])\n```\n\niii. The AI explicitly documented this decision: \"Use all clusters (qc=None): Matches reference code, not just well-isolated neurons.\" The reference code's `load_spiking_data()` is called without the `qc` parameter in `prepare_data()`, defaulting to `qc=None` which returns all clusters.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to stimulus onset (`stimOn_times`). For each trial, the binning window starts at `stimOn_times - 0.5` and ends at `stimOn_times + 1.5`.\n\nii.\n```python\nALIGN_TIME = 'stimOn_times'\nTIME_WINDOW = (-0.5, 1.5)\n\nalign_times = valid_trials[ALIGN_TIME].values\ninterval_starts = align_times + TIME_WINDOW[0]\ninterval_ends = align_times + TIME_WINDOW[1]\n```\n\niii. Matches the reference code's `0_data_caching.py` parameters: `align_time='stimOn_times'`, `time_window=(-.5, 1.5)`, and the task specification which says \"Temporally align based on stimulus onset.\"\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The temporal resolution is 20ms (0.02s), producing 100 time bins per trial. No rebinning is applied; spikes are directly binned at 20ms resolution.\n\nii.\n```python\nBINSIZE = 0.02  # 20 ms time bins\nN_BINS = int(np.ceil((TIME_WINDOW[1] - TIME_WINDOW[0]) / BINSIZE))  # 100\n```\n\niii. Matches the reference code's `binsize=0.02` and the methods paper's \"20-ms bins.\"\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time since stimulus onset* derived from?\n\ni. Time since stimulus onset is not derived from any raw data variable. It is synthetically constructed from the time window parameters and bin size, since it is the same for every trial by definition.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_since_stim = np.linspace(TIME_WINDOW[0] + BINSIZE, TIME_WINDOW[1], N_BINS).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The AI recognized that this input is deterministic given the alignment to stimulus onset. The formula `linspace(-0.48, 1.5, 100)` matches the reference code's interpolation grid: `x_interp = np.linspace(interval_beg + binsize, interval_end, n_bins)`.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since stimulus onset*?\n\ni. A linearly spaced array from -0.48s to 1.5s with 100 points is created. The start is offset by one bin size (0.02s) from the window start (-0.5s), matching the reference code's behavior interpolation grid convention.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_since_stim = np.linspace(TIME_WINDOW[0] + BINSIZE, TIME_WINDOW[1], N_BINS).astype(np.float32)\n# Result: array from -0.48 to 1.5, 100 evenly spaced values\n```\n\niii. This follows the same convention as the reference code's `get_behavior_per_interval()` interpolation grid.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time since stimulus onset* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. The time array has the same number of bins (100) as the neural data, and both cover the same time window (-0.5 to 1.5s). The time array is broadcast identically across all trials as the first row of the input matrix.\n\nii.\n```python\ninp = np.array([\n    time_since_stim,  # time-varying\n    np.full(N_BINS, trial_in_block_final[t], dtype=np.float32),\n], dtype=np.float32)  # (2, n_bins)\n```\n\niii. Alignment is implicit: both neural and input have 100 time bins spanning the same window.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Trial number in block* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `probabilityLeft` column of the trials table. Block boundaries are detected where `probabilityLeft` changes value.\n\nii.\n```python\nprob_left_all = trials['probabilityLeft'].values\ntrial_in_block_all = compute_trial_in_block(prob_left_all)\n```\n\niii. The AI noted: \"Block boundaries are where probabilityLeft changes.\" This is a reasonable approach since probabilityLeft directly encodes the block identity (0.2, 0.5, or 0.8).\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Trial number in block*?\n\ni. Iterates through all trials in order. A counter starts at 1 and increments each trial. When `probabilityLeft` changes, the counter resets to 1. The computation is done on ALL trials first, then the appropriate values are selected for filtered/valid trials. The result is broadcast to all 100 time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef compute_trial_in_block(prob_left):\n    trial_in_block = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=np.float32)\n    count = 1\n    for i in range(len(prob_left)):\n        if i > 0 and prob_left[i] != prob_left[i-1]:\n            count = 1\n        trial_in_block[i] = count\n        count += 1\n    return trial_in_block\n\n# Applied to all trials, then masked\ntrial_in_block_all = compute_trial_in_block(prob_left_all)\ntrial_in_block_valid = trial_in_block_all[mask.values]\ntrial_in_block_final = trial_in_block_valid[combined_mask]\n```\n\niii. Computing on all trials before filtering ensures accurate block boundary detection and trial counting.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `choice` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice = final_trials['choice'].values.copy()\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference code which reads `trials_df['choice'].to_numpy()`.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Choice*?\n\ni. IBL encodes choice as -1 (left) and 1 (right). The AI converts to 0 (left) and 1 (right) using `((choice + 1) // 2)`. No-choice trials (choice == 0) are already excluded by the trial mask. The result is broadcast to all 100 time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice_encoded = ((choice + 1) // 2).astype(int)  # -1->0, 1->1\n\n# Broadcast to time bins in output\nnp.full(N_BINS, choice_encoded[t], dtype=int),  # per-trial, broadcast\n```\n\niii. Matches the task specification: \"Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1.\"\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Prior probability of left* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `probabilityLeft` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\nprob_left = final_trials['probabilityLeft'].values\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference code which reads `trials_df['probabilityLeft']`.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Prior probability of left*?\n\ni. Direct categorical mapping: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. The result is broadcast to all 100 time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\nprior_encoded = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=int)\nprior_encoded[prob_left == 0.2] = 0\nprior_encoded[prob_left == 0.5] = 1\nprior_encoded[prob_left == 0.8] = 2\n\n# Broadcast in output\nnp.full(N_BINS, prior_encoded[t], dtype=int),\n```\n\niii. Matches the task specification: \"Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2.\"\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Wheel speed* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `_ibl_wheel.position.npy` (wheel position) and `_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy` (timestamps).\n\nii.\n```python\nwheel_pos_raw = np.load(find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_wheel.position.npy')).flatten()\nwheel_ts_raw = np.load(find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy')).flatten()\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference code which loads wheel data via `SessionLoader.load_wheel()`.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. Wheel position is interpolated to 1000 Hz uniform sampling, then filtered with a Butterworth low-pass filter (order 8, corner frequency 20 Hz), velocity computed as `np.diff(filtered) * fs`, and absolute value taken for speed.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_wheel(timestamps, position, fs=1000, corner_freq=20, order=8):\n    t = np.arange(timestamps[0], timestamps[-1], 1.0 / fs)\n    pos_interp = interp1d(timestamps, position, kind='linear')(t)\n    sos = signal.butter(N=order, Wn=corner_freq / fs * 2, btype='lowpass', output='sos')\n    vel = np.insert(np.diff(signal.sosfiltfilt(sos, pos_interp)), 0, 0) * fs\n    return t, np.abs(vel).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. This exactly replicates the reference code's `velocity_filtered()` from ibllib: same interpolation frequency (1000 Hz), same Butterworth filter parameters (order=8, corner=20 Hz), same velocity computation, same absolute value for speed.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Wheel speed is discretized into 3 equal-frequency (quantile) bins per session. Quantile boundaries at 0%, 33.3%, 66.7%, 100% are computed across all valid timepoints in the session, then `np.digitize` assigns each value to a bin (0=low, 1=medium, 2=high).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_to_bins(values, n_bins=3):\n    valid = values[~np.isnan(values)]\n    quantiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\n    boundaries = np.percentile(valid, quantiles)\n    result = np.digitize(values, boundaries[1:-1])\n    result = np.clip(result, 0, n_bins - 1)\n    return result.astype(int), boundaries\n\nall_wheel_flat = final_wheel.flatten()\nwheel_disc, wheel_boundaries = discretize_to_bins(all_wheel_flat, N_DISCRETE_BINS)\nwheel_disc_2d = wheel_disc.reshape(final_wheel.shape).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. The task spec says \"Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins\" without specifying the method. The AI chose equal-frequency quantile bins computed per session. This ensures balanced classes within each session but means bin boundaries differ across sessions.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Wheel speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Continuous wheel speed is interpolated to trial-aligned bins using the same time grid as neural data: `linspace(t_start + binsize, t_end, n_bins)` where t_start/t_end are defined by stimOn_times +/- the time window.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_behavior_to_bins(beh_times, beh_values, interval_starts, interval_ends, binsize, n_bins):\n    x_interp = np.linspace(t_start + binsize, t_end, n_bins)\n    y_interp = interp1d(t_sel, v_sel, kind='linear', fill_value='extrapolate')(x_interp)\n    result[trial_idx] = y_interp\n```\n\niii. Computing on all trials before filtering ensures accurate block boundary detection and trial counting.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Choice* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `choice` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice = final_trials['choice'].values.copy()\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference code which reads `trials_df['choice'].to_numpy()`.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Choice*?\n\ni. IBL encodes choice as -1 (left) and 1 (right). The AI converts to 0 (left) and 1 (right) using `((choice + 1) // 2)`. No-choice trials (choice == 0) are already excluded by the trial mask. The result is broadcast to all 100 time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\nchoice_encoded = ((choice + 1) // 2).astype(int)  # -1->0, 1->1\n\n# Broadcast to time bins in output\nnp.full(N_BINS, choice_encoded[t], dtype=int),  # per-trial, broadcast\n```\n\niii. Matches the task specification: \"Choice, binary, per-trial, left = 0, right = 1.\"\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Prior probability of left* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the `probabilityLeft` column in `_ibl_trials.table.pqt`.\n\nii.\n```python\nprob_left = final_trials['probabilityLeft'].values\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference code which reads `trials_df['probabilityLeft']`.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Prior probability of left*?\n\ni. Direct categorical mapping: 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2. The result is broadcast to all 100 time bins as a per-trial constant.\n\nii.\n```python\nprior_encoded = np.zeros(len(prob_left), dtype=int)\nprior_encoded[prob_left == 0.2] = 0\nprior_encoded[prob_left == 0.5] = 1\nprior_encoded[prob_left == 0.8] = 2\n\n# Broadcast in output\nnp.full(N_BINS, prior_encoded[t], dtype=int),\n```\n\niii. Matches the task specification: \"Prior probability of left, per-trial, 0.2 -> 0, 0.5 -> 1, 0.8 -> 2.\"\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Wheel speed* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `_ibl_wheel.position.npy` (wheel position) and `_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy` (timestamps).\n\nii.\n```python\nwheel_pos_raw = np.load(find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_wheel.position.npy')).flatten()\nwheel_ts_raw = np.load(find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_wheel.timestamps.npy')).flatten()\n```\n\niii. Same source as the reference code which loads wheel data via `SessionLoader.load_wheel()`.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Wheel speed*?\n\ni. Wheel position is interpolated to 1000 Hz uniform sampling, then filtered with a Butterworth low-pass filter (order 8, corner frequency 20 Hz), velocity computed as `np.diff(filtered) * fs`, and absolute value taken for speed.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_wheel(timestamps, position, fs=1000, corner_freq=20, order=8):\n    t = np.arange(timestamps[0], timestamps[-1], 1.0 / fs)\n    pos_interp = interp1d(timestamps, position, kind='linear')(t)\n    sos = signal.butter(N=order, Wn=corner_freq / fs * 2, btype='lowpass', output='sos')\n    vel = np.insert(np.diff(signal.sosfiltfilt(sos, pos_interp)), 0, 0) * fs\n    return t, np.abs(vel).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. This exactly replicates the reference code's `velocity_filtered()` from ibllib: same interpolation frequency (1000 Hz), same Butterworth filter parameters (order=8, corner=20 Hz), same velocity computation, same absolute value for speed.\n\n## 7-c. How is `output` *Wheel speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Wheel speed is discretized into 3 equal-frequency (quantile) bins per session. Quantile boundaries at 0%, 33.3%, 66.7%, 100% are computed across all valid timepoints in the session, then `np.digitize` assigns each value to a bin (0=low, 1=medium, 2=high).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef discretize_to_bins(values, n_bins=3):\n    valid = values[~np.isnan(values)]\n    quantiles = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)\n    boundaries = np.percentile(valid, quantiles)\n    result = np.digitize(values, boundaries[1:-1])\n    result = np.clip(result, 0, n_bins - 1)\n    return result.astype(int), boundaries\n\nall_wheel_flat = final_wheel.flatten()\nwheel_disc, wheel_boundaries = discretize_to_bins(all_wheel_flat, N_DISCRETE_BINS)\nwheel_disc_2d = wheel_disc.reshape(final_wheel.shape).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. The task spec says \"Wheel speed discretized into 3 bins\" without specifying the method. The AI chose equal-frequency quantile bins computed per session. This ensures balanced classes within each session but means bin boundaries differ across sessions.\n\n## 7-d. How is `output` *Wheel speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Continuous wheel speed is interpolated to trial-aligned bins using the same time grid as neural data: `linspace(t_start + binsize, t_end, n_bins)` where t_start/t_end are defined by stimOn_times +/- the time window.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef interpolate_behavior_to_bins(beh_times, beh_values, interval_starts, interval_ends, binsize, n_bins):\n    x_interp = np.linspace(t_start + binsize, t_end, n_bins)\n    y_interp = interp1d(t_sel, v_sel, kind='linear', fill_value='extrapolate')(x_interp)\n    result[trial_idx] = y_interp\n```\n\niii. Matches the reference code's `get_behavior_per_interval()` which uses the identical interpolation grid and method (linear interpolation with extrapolation).\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Whisker motion energy* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy` (motion energy values) and `_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy` (timestamps). Falls back to right camera if left is unavailable.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_motion_energy(alf_path, side='left'):\n    if side == 'left':\n        me_file = find_latest_revision(alf_path, 'leftCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy')\n        times_file = find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_leftCamera.times.npy')\n    else:\n        me_file = find_latest_revision(alf_path, 'rightCamera.ROIMotionEnergy.npy')\n        times_file = find_latest_revision(alf_path, '_ibl_rightCamera.times.npy')\n\nme_times, me_values = load_motion_energy(alf_path, side='left')\nif me_times is None:\n    me_times, me_values = load_motion_energy(alf_path, side='right')\n```\n\niii. Matches the reference code's `bin_behaviors()` which tries left whisker ME first and falls back to right.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Whisker motion energy*?\n\ni. Motion energy values are loaded directly from the numpy files with no additional processing (no filtering, no normalization). Length mismatch between timestamps and values is handled by truncating to the shorter length.\n\nii.\n```python\nme = np.load(me_file).flatten()\ntimes = np.load(times_file).flatten()\nmin_len = min(len(me), len(times))\nreturn times[:min_len], me[:min_len].astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Matches the reference code which loads whisker ME directly via `sess_loader.motion_energy['leftCamera']['whiskerMotionEnergy']` without additional processing.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Same method as wheel speed: discretized into 3 equal-frequency quantile bins per session.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_me_flat = final_me.flatten()\nme_disc, me_boundaries = discretize_to_bins(all_me_flat, N_DISCRETE_BINS)\nme_disc_2d = me_disc.reshape(final_me.shape).astype(int)\n```\n\niii. Same rationale as wheel speed discretization (7-c).\n\n## 8-d. How is `output` *Whisker motion energy* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same interpolation method as wheel speed: continuous motion energy interpolated to the trial-aligned bin grid using linear interpolation with extrapolation.\n\nii.\n```python\nme_binned, me_good = interpolate_behavior_to_bins(\n    me_times, me_values, interval_starts, interval_ends, BINSIZE, N_BINS\n)\n```\n\niii. Uses the same `interpolate_behavior_to_bins()` function as wheel speed, matching the reference code's approach.\n\n## 9. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Multiple levels of missing data handling:\n- Sessions with missing file paths are skipped entirely.\n- Probes that fail to load are skipped (session continues if at least one probe succeeds).\n- Trials with NaN in key event columns are excluded by the trial mask.\n- Trials without valid wheel or motion energy data are excluded by the combined mask.\n- Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials after all filtering are skipped.\n- Camera timestamp/value length mismatches are handled by truncating to the shorter length.\n- Revision directories are searched for the latest revision of each file.\n\nii.\n```python\n# Session path check\nif alf_path is None:\n    print(f\"  Session {eid}: path not found, skipping\")\n    return None\n\n# Probe error handling\ntry:\n    spk, clu = load_spike_sorting(alf_path, probe_name)\nexcept Exception as e:\n    print(f\"  Session {eid}, {probe_name}: error loading spikes: {e}\")\n    continue\n\n# Minimum trial check\nif n_valid < 2:\n    print(f\"  Session {eid}: fewer than 2 trials with all data, skipping\")\n    return None\n\n# Camera length mismatch\nmin_len = min(len(me), len(times))\nreturn times[:min_len], me[:min_len]\n```\n\niii.
